=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
Tiles are laid out covering the two-dimensional coordinate plane.
Each tile is a rectangle, and for each integer triple (i, j, k) satisfying 0 \leq k < K, a corresponding tile is placed according to the following rules:

- When i and j have the same parity (both even or both odd), the tile corresponding to (i, j, k) covers the area where iK \leq x \leq (i + 1)K and jK + k \leq y \leq jK + k + 1.
- When i and j have different parity, the tile corresponding to (i, j, k) covers the area where iK + k \leq x \leq iK + k + 1 and jK \leq y \leq (j + 1)K.

Two tiles are adjacent when their edges have a common segment of positive length.
Starting from the tile containing the point (S_x + 0.5, S_y + 0.5), find the minimum number of times you need to move to an adjacent tile to reach the tile containing the point (T_x + 0.5, T_y + 0.5).
There are T test cases; solve each of them.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
T
\text{case}_1
\vdots
\text{case}_T

Each case is given in the following format:
K S_x S_y T_x T_y

Output

Print T lines. The i-th line should contain the answer for the i-th test case.

Constraints


- 1 \leq T \leq 10^4
- 2 \leq K \leq 10^{16}
- -10^{16} \leq S_x, S_y, T_x, T_y \leq 10^{16}
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

3
3 -2 1 4 -1
4 8 8 0 2
5 -1000000000000 -1000000000000 1000000000000 1000000000000

Sample Output 1

4
4
800000000000

Let us explain the first test case.
Let (i, j, k) denote the tile corresponding to integer triple (i, j, k).
(-1.5, 1.5) is contained in tile (-1, 0, 1), and (4.5, -0.5) is contained in tile (1, -1, 2).
For example, by moving from tile (-1, 0, 1) to (-1, 0, 2) to (0, 0, 2) to (1, 0, 0) to (1, -1, 2), you can reach tile (1, -1, 2) in four moves to an adjacent tile.

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's list adjacencies more compactly using "ports" at block boundaries:
- Along vertical boundary between H block (I,J) and V block (I+1,J) (H left, V right): H_k (all k) adjacent to V_0. This is a K-fold edge between H_k and V_0? Actually V_0 adjacent to all H_k. So in graph, V_0 is connected to all H_k in left H block. Similarly V_{K-1} connected to all H_k in right H block.
- Along horizontal boundary between H block (I,J) and V block (I,J+1) (H below? If H at (I,J), V above (I,J+1)): H_{K-1} top edge adjacent to V_k for all k. So H_{K-1} connected to all V_k in above V block. H_0 connected to all V_k in below V block.
Thus each boundary between an H block and a V block is a complete bipartite connection between one "boundary tile" of one block and all tiles of the other block? Let's see:
- Vertical boundary H left, V right: V_0 (boundary tile of V block) connected to all H_k. So one V tile connected to all H tiles.
- Vertical boundary V left, H right: V_{K-1} connected to all H_k.
- Horizontal boundary H below, V above: H_{K-1} connected to all V_k.
- Horizontal boundary H above, V below: H_0 connected to all V_k.

Thus every edge between blocks is a star: one special tile on one side connected to all K tiles on the other side. Also within H/V blocks, adjacent k chain.

This is reminiscent of graph Cartesian product? Maybe each block has K "states" (k). Boundary transitions:
- From H block to neighboring V block horizontally: regardless of k, you can go to V_0 (right) or V_{K-1} (left). So horizontal boundary is "all-to-one" from H to V.
- From V block to neighboring H block vertically: regardless of k, you can go to H_0 (up) or H_{K-1} (down). So vertical boundary is all-to-one from V to H.
- The reverse directions are one-to-all from the special tile to all tiles in the other block.

This asymmetry may allow a path to choose k freely when entering a block from a boundary where the special tile is on the other side? Let's formalize.

When moving from H block to adjacent V block horizontally:
- H_k -> V_0 (right) or V_{K-1} (left). The resulting V tile has fixed k=0 or K-1. So horizontal H->V sets k to boundary value.
When moving from V block to adjacent H block vertically:
- V_k -> H_0 (up) or H_{K-1} (down). Resulting H tile has fixed k=0 or K-1. So vertical V->H sets k to boundary value.

When moving from V block to adjacent H block horizontally:
- Only if V_k with k=0 can go left to any H_l; or if k=K-1 can go right to any H_l. So requires V k at boundary, and then can choose any H k.
When moving from H block to adjacent V block vertically:
- Only if H_k with k=0 can go down to any V_l; or if k=K-1 can go up to any V_l. Requires H k at boundary, then choose any V k.

Thus the graph is directed in terms of "control" of k: Some moves are forced to boundary k; some moves require boundary k but allow arbitrary k after.

This suggests shortest path can be modeled by "states" representing whether current tile is at a boundary k=0/K-1 or interior, and direction of travel. But K huge; interior distance to boundary matters.

Maybe we can think of each tile's "k" as a coordinate along the strip direction. To move through a block in the direction perpendicular to its strips? Let's identify:
- H block strips are horizontal. Moving vertically within H block changes k (cost |Δk|). Moving horizontally through H block to neighboring V blocks is possible from any k (cost1 to boundary V), but the V tile is at boundary k=0/K-1.
- V block strips are vertical. Moving horizontally within V block changes k. Moving vertically through V block to neighboring H blocks is possible from any k (cost1 to boundary H_0/H_{K-1}).

So each block has a "long" direction (width K for H horizontal, height K for V vertical) and a "short" direction (height 1 for H, width1 for V). Actually H tile is long horizontally, short vertically; V tile is long vertically, short horizontally. Within a block, moving in the short direction changes k (the strip index). So horizontal movement through H block is like a "corridor" at any k, cost2 to cross from V to V, preserving k if no internal change. Let's define a "state" as (I,J,o,k). We can think of moving in block grid. Since K huge, but k can be compressed to three categories: 0, K-1, interior. Let's analyze the graph more systematically using "diagonal moves" as basic steps.

Observation: From any H tile, you can move to any diagonally adjacent H tile in 2 moves, but the target k is either 0 or K-1 depending on the route chosen? Let's enumerate all possible 2-move H->H diagonal transitions and target k possibilities.

H(I,J,k) -> V neighbor -> H diagonal neighbor.

Neighbor V options:
1. V_right = V(I+1,J,0). From V_right:
   - top -> H(I+1,J+1,0) (NE target 0)
   - bottom -> H(I+1,J-1,K-1) (SE target K-1)
   - left -> H(I,J,k') (back same block, any k') (not diagonal)
   - internal -> V(I+1,J,1) (not H)
2. V_left = V(I-1,J,K-1). From V_left:
   - top -> H(I-1,J+1,0) (NW target 0)
   - bottom -> H(I-1,J-1,K-1) (SW target K-1)
   - right -> H(I,J,k') (back)
3. V_bottom? H can go to V(I,J-1,l) only if k=0. From V(I,J-1,l):
   - if l=K-1: right -> H(I+1,J-1,k') (SE target any)
   - if l=0: left -> H(I-1,J-1,k') (SW target any)
   - top -> H(I,J,0) back (if l? V top to H_0)
   - internal etc.
4. V_top? if k=K-1: V(I,J+1,l):
   - if l=K-1: right -> H(I+1,J+1,k') (NE target any)
   - if l=0: left -> H(I-1,J+1,k') (NW target any)

Thus H->H diagonal 2-move transitions:
- To NE (I+1,J+1):
  * from any k: target 0 (via V_right top)
  * if k=K-1: target any (via V_top right)
- To NW (I-1,J+1):
  * from any k: target 0 (via V_left top)
  * if k=K-1: target any (via V_top left)
- To SE (I+1,J-1):
  * from any k: target K-1 (via V_right bottom)
  * if k=0: target any (via V_bottom right)
- To SW (I-1,J-1):
  * from any k: target K-1 (via V_left bottom)
  * if k=0: target any (via V_bottom left)

Similarly V->V diagonal 2-move transitions:
V(I,J,k) -> H neighbor -> V diagonal.
Neighbor H options:
1. H_top = H(I,J+1,0). From H_top:
   - right -> V(I+1,J+1,0) (NE target 0)
   - left -> V(I-1,J+1,K-1) (NW target K-1)
   - bottom -> V(I,J,k') back (any k')
2. H_bottom = H(I,J-1,K-1). From H_bottom:
   - right -> V(I+1,J-1,0) (SE target 0)
   - left -> V(I-1,J-1,K-1) (SW target K-1)
   - top -> V(I,J,k') back
3. H_left? V can go to H(I-1,J,l) only if k=0. From H(I-1,J,l):
   - if l=K-1: top -> V(I-1,J+1,k') (NW target any)
   - if l=0: bottom -> V(I-1,J-1,k') (SW target any)
   - right -> V(I,J,0) back
4. H_right? if k=K-1: H(I+1,J,l):
   - if l=K-1: top -> V(I+1,J+1,k') (NE target any)
   - if l=0: bottom -> V(I+1,J-1,k') (SE target any)

Thus V->V diagonal:
- To NE (I+1,J+1):
  * from any k: target 0 (via H_top right)
  * if k=K-1: target any (via H_right top)
- To SE (I+1,J-1):
  * from any k: target 0 (via H_bottom right)
  * if k=K-1: target any (via H_right bottom)
- To NW (I-1,J+1):
  * from any k: target K-1 (via H_top left)
  * if k=0: target any (via H_left top)
- To SW (I-1,J-1):
  * from any k: target K-1 (via H_bottom left)
  * if k=0: target any (via H_left bottom)

This symmetry: H target k depends on vertical direction; V target k depends on horizontal direction. Boundary k allows arbitrary target in certain directions.

Also orthogonal H<->V moves:
H(I,J,k) -> V(I+1,J,0) (right) target k=0
H(I,J,k) -> V(I-1,J,K-1) (left) target k=K-1
H(I,J,0) -> V(I,J-1,l) any l (down)
H(I,J,K-1) -> V(I,J+1,l) any l (up)

V(I,J,k) -> H(I,J+1,0) (up) target k=0
V(I,J,k) -> H(I,J-1,K-1) (down) target k=K-1
V(I,J,0) -> H(I-1,J,l) any l (left)
V(I,J,K-1) -> H(I+1,J,l) any l (right)

So orthogonal moves can be seen as:
- H to V horizontally: from any k, target V k = 0 (east) or K-1 (west).
- V to H vertically: from any k, target H k = 0 (north) or K-1 (south).
- H to V vertically: requires H k=0 (south) or K-1 (north), target V k arbitrary.
- V to H horizontally: requires V k=0 (west) or K-1 (east), target H k arbitrary.

This is like a "directed" graph where one direction is forced, reverse is free if at boundary.

Another perspective: The adjacency graph is the dual of a rectangular tiling. The distance between two rectangles equals the minimum number of rectangle boundaries crossed. Let's model the problem as shortest path in a graph where each tile is a node. Another approach: Because all moves are between rectangles, maybe the distance equals the length of the shortest path in the "block grid" with edge weights 1, but where each tile has a "height" coordinate k that can be adjusted at cost 1. This is like moving a token on a 3D grid (I,J,k,o) with moves:
- H: (I,J,k) <-> (I,J,k±1) cost1; (I,J,k) <-> (I±1,J, V, k=0/K-1) cost1; etc.
Maybe we can eliminate k by considering it as a third coordinate with periodic boundaries? Let's define a continuous coordinate:
For H tile, k is y-offset; for V tile, k is x-offset. The tile can be represented by its "lower-left" corner (x0,y0) = (IK, JK+k) for H, (IK+k, JK) for V. Adjacent moves change x0/y0 by? Let's compute in terms of lower-left corners:

So adjacency between H and V occurs when one tile's interval along the shared axis contains the other's interval:
- Vertical boundary: H's y-interval (length1) is contained in V's y-interval (length K). So H_k adjacent to V_0/V_{K-1} for all k.
- Horizontal boundary: V's x-interval (length1) is contained in H's x-interval (length K). So V_l adjacent to H_0/H_{K-1} for all l.

This containment perspective may help. Each H tile has a small y-interval; each V tile has a small x-interval. The tiling is like a grid of intervals where H and V tiles are "crosses".

- V tile: x-interval unit [IK+k, IK+k+1], y-interval full block [JK,(J+1)K].

Adjacency between H and V occurs if their intervals overlap in one dimension and touch in the other. Specifically:
- If H is left of V: H.x right = V.x left, and H.y interval overlaps V.y interval (always because V.y full block contains H.y). So H_k adjacent to V_0 in right block.
- If H is right of V: H.x left = V.x right, overlap y.
- If H is below V: H.y top = V.y bottom, and H.x interval overlaps V.x interval (always because H.x full block contains V.x). So H_{K-1} adjacent to V_l above.
- If H is above V: H.y bottom = V.y top.

This is exactly interval containment.

Let's attempt to assign coordinates by considering the "rank" of the tile's interval in a laminar family. The x-intervals of tiles are either full blocks (H) or unit strips (V). The y-intervals are unit strips (H) or full blocks (V). This is like a product of two "alternating" interval systems.

Let's consider the set of vertical lines that separate tiles. A vertical line x = n can be:
- A block boundary at n = iK. Along this line, H and V tiles alternate in y: for each unit y interval, there is a boundary between H_k and V_{K-1}/V_0. Crossing this line changes block index by 1 and orientation.
- An internal V boundary at n = iK + k (1..K-1). Along this line, V_{k-1} and V_k share a vertical edge of length K. Crossing this line changes k by ±1 within a V block.
- Inside H tiles, vertical lines are not boundaries.

Similarly horizontal lines:
- Block boundary y = jK: H/V boundary.
- Internal H boundary y = jK+k: H_{k-1}/H_k boundary.

Thus the "walls" are vertical lines at x = iK (full block boundaries) and x = iK+k for k=1..K-1 inside V blocks; horizontal lines at y = jK and y = jK+k inside H blocks.

This is like a grid of "streets" where vertical streets exist at all multiples of K, plus at offsets k inside V blocks; horizontal streets at all multiples of K, plus offsets k inside H blocks.

Let's derive a general method to compute distance using "minimum over monotone paths with k adjustments" perhaps.

Because any path can be transformed to a path of length block Manhattan + extra where extra is small. We can compute the minimal extra by considering the source and target "boundary states" and the direction of travel.

Maybe we can model the path at the level of block moves (N/S/E/W) and k. Since internal moves can be inserted to adjust k, but we can assume all internal adjustments happen at source or target or at turning points? In a shortest path, extra moves beyond Manhattan are either:
- internal moves at some block to change k to satisfy next move,
- detours (extra orthogonal moves) that effectively change k by 2 without changing net block displacement.

But because k can be changed from any value to any other within a block at cost at most 2 (via adjacent opposite block detour), the cost of adjusting k at a block is at most 2. However if you need to adjust k multiple times along a long path, maybe you can choose a path that avoids adjustments. The total extra might be the minimum number of "k mismatches" along the monotone path, each costing 0,1,2? But a monotone path has no internal moves, so k is determined by the sequence of moves. If at some step the required k doesn't match, you could insert an internal adjustment at the current block costing |Δk| or 2. Since you can adjust to any k at cost ≤2, each mismatch costs at most 2. But if multiple mismatches, extra could accumulate. However maybe you can choose a different monotone path (order of N/S/E/W) to avoid mismatches. The number of monotone paths is binomial; for large Δ, many choices. We need compute if there exists a monotone path with zero mismatches; if not, minimal mismatches.

Let's characterize k evolution along a monotone path (only orthogonal moves, no internal).

We have two orientations. Let's track k after each move.

Rules for orthogonal moves:
1. H --E--> V: V k = 0. (requires nothing)
2. H --W--> V: V k = K-1.
3. H --N--> V: requires H k = K-1; V k arbitrary (choose).
4. H --S--> V: requires H k = 0; V k arbitrary.
5. V --N--> H: H k = 0. (requires nothing)
6. V --S--> H: H k = K-1.
7. V --E--> H: requires V k = K-1; H k arbitrary.
8. V --W--> H: requires V k = 0; H k arbitrary.

Notice:
- Horizontal moves H->V set V k to 0 (E) or K-1 (W).
- Vertical moves V->H set H k to 0 (N) or K-1 (S).
- The reverse moves (V->H horizontal, H->V vertical) require the moving tile's k to be boundary (0/K-1) but allow arbitrary target k.

Thus along a monotone path, whenever you move from H to V horizontally, the V k is forced to 0 or K-1. Whenever you move from V to H vertically, H k forced to 0 or K-1. When you move V->H horizontally, you need V k boundary but can set H k arbitrary. When H->V vertically, need H k boundary but can set V k arbitrary.

This is like a path in a directed graph where k is either forced or free. We can think of each tile as having a "free coordinate" that can be chosen when entering through a long side, but must be boundary to exit through a short side.

Maybe we can compute the set of possible k values at each block along a monotone path. Since k can be arbitrary after certain moves, the exact value may be chosen to satisfy future requirements. This is a constraint satisfaction problem along a path. For a given sequence of moves, the k values at H/V nodes are variables with constraints:
- H node: if outgoing move is N, k=K-1; if S, k=0; if E/W, no constraint (but incoming may set k).
- V node: if outgoing move is E, k=K-1; if W, k=0; if N/S, no constraint.
- Incoming to H from N (V below -> H): k=0; from S (V above -> H): k=K-1; from E/W (V left/right -> H): k arbitrary (no constraint).
- Incoming to V from E (H left -> V): k=0; from W (H right -> V): k=K-1; from N/S (H below/above -> V): k arbitrary.

So constraints only involve k at a node if the path enters/exits through certain sides. If both incoming and outgoing impose constraints, they must match or be adjustable? In a monotone path without internal moves, no adjustment, so constraints must be compatible. If incompatible, need extra internal move at that node (cost) or choose different path.

This is similar to routing in a grid with "ports" at tile sides. The k at a tile is the position along the short side. Entering through a long side sets it arbitrarily; exiting through a short side requires it at an end. If you enter through a short side, k is forced; then exiting through the opposite short side may require the other end, causing mismatch.

Let's classify tile sides:
For H tile:
- Long sides: left/right (vertical edges). Entering from left/right V gives arbitrary k. Exiting to left/right V is always allowed from any k, but sets V k to boundary (0/K-1) depending on direction. So H's long sides are "free entry, free exit" (exit doesn't require H k boundary).
- Short sides: bottom/top. Entering from bottom/top V forces k=0/K-1. Exiting to bottom/top V requires k=0/K-1.
Thus H tile is like a horizontal corridor: you can pass horizontally at any k; to turn vertically, you must be at the appropriate end.

For V tile:
- Long sides: top/bottom. Entering from top/bottom H gives arbitrary k; exiting to top/bottom H is always allowed from any k, but sets H k to boundary.
- Short sides: left/right. Entering from left/right H forces k=0/K-1. Exiting to left/right H requires k=0/K-1.

So each tile is a rectangle with two long sides and two short sides. A path through a tile can go:
- straight through long sides (H horizontal, V vertical): no k constraint, k preserved.
- turn from long side to short side: if entering long (k arbitrary), to exit short need k=0/K-1. This may require internal adjustment if k not boundary.
- turn from short side to long side: entering short forces k boundary, exiting long allowed, so k boundary.
- straight through short sides (H vertical, V horizontal): entering short forces one boundary, exiting opposite short requires other boundary, so mismatch unless K=2? Wait H entering bottom forces k=0, exiting top requires k=K-1. Without internal adjustment, impossible for K>2. But a monotone path of length block Manhattan would never go straight through short sides? Actually moving north through an H block means enter from bottom V (short side) and exit top V (short side), requiring k=0 then K-1. That is impossible in one visit without internal moves. But in the block grid, moving N from H to V above and then N again from V to H? Wait a path in block coordinates alternates H and V blocks. A "straight through short sides" of an H tile would correspond to two consecutive vertical moves: V below -> H -> V above. In the block path, the H node has incoming from south and outgoing north. This requires k=0 on entry and k=K-1 on exit. Without internal adjustment, impossible for K>2. Therefore a monotone path cannot contain a pattern V->H->V with both vertical moves (i.e., moving straight north through an H block). Similarly H->V->H with both horizontal moves through a V block is impossible. This is key! In a monotone path, you cannot go straight through a block in the direction perpendicular to its strips; you must turn or adjust.

For H block, strips are horizontal, so vertical pass is "short-to-short" and impossible without internal moves. Horizontal pass is "long-to-long" and easy. For V block, horizontal pass impossible, vertical pass easy.

Thus a monotone path (no internal moves) must avoid going straight through a block in its "hard" direction:
- H block: cannot have consecutive vertical moves (N then N or S then S) through it? Actually if path enters H from south and leaves north, that's two vertical moves with H in between. More generally, at an H node, if incoming direction and outgoing direction are both vertical (N/S), impossible. If one vertical and one horizontal, possible if k boundary matches? Let's check:
  - Enter H from south (bottom) forces k=0. Exit H east/west (long side) allowed from any k, so possible. So turn from vertical to horizontal is possible.
  - Enter H from east/west (long side) arbitrary k. Exit H north requires k=K-1; may need k=K-1. If arbitrary chosen as K-1, possible. So turn from horizontal to vertical possible by choosing k on entry.
  - Enter H from south (k=0), exit north (k=K-1) impossible without internal.
  - Enter H from north (k=K-1), exit south (k=0) impossible.
  - Enter/exit both horizontal: possible, k preserved.
- V block: cannot have consecutive horizontal moves (E then E or W then W) through it? Actually V node incoming from west/east (short sides) and outgoing east/west (short sides) impossible. Turns between horizontal and vertical possible.

So a monotone path is feasible iff it never goes straight through a block in its hard direction. That is, the path in the block grid cannot have two consecutive steps in the same direction if that direction is vertical through an H block or horizontal through a V block. But since block orientation alternates with parity, what does this mean in terms of step sequence?

Let's represent the path as a sequence of moves in {N,S,E,W}. At each step, the current block orientation toggles. The "hard direction" for a block depends on its orientation:
- H block (even parity): hard directions are N/S (vertical). So if the path enters an H block and leaves in a vertical direction, that's a straight vertical pass through H, impossible.
- V block (odd parity): hard directions are E/W. So straight horizontal pass through V impossible.

But note the path visits blocks; a straight pass through a block corresponds to two consecutive moves in the same axis (e.g., E then E, or N then N) with the intermediate block being the one passed through. Actually to go from block A to B to C, if B is H and A->B and B->C are both vertical, then B is passed vertically. This means the two moves are in the same vertical direction? Could be N then N (A below B, C above B) or S then S. If A->B is N and B->C is N, yes same direction. If A->B is S and B->C is S, same. If A->B is N and B->C is S, that would go back to A, not a monotone path (unless extra). So straight pass is two consecutive moves in the same direction (N,N or S,S) for vertical, or E,E/W,W for horizontal.

Thus a monotone path (only moving towards target, no backtracking) consists of a sequence of E/W moves and N/S moves, but never two consecutive moves in the same direction? Wait if you move E then E, you pass through a V block horizontally (because after first E, you're in a V block; second E leaves it to the east). That is hard for V. So E,E is forbidden. Similarly N,N forbidden (passes through H vertically). What about E then N? At the intermediate V block, incoming from west (short side) and outgoing north (long side) is a turn, allowed if V k=0? This is promising. Let's formalize.

A path in the tile graph can be projected to a walk in the block grid. Moves that change block are orthogonal steps; internal moves stay in same block. A shortest path will have a projected walk from source block to target block. The number of block-changing moves is at least block Manhattan M = |ΔI|+|ΔJ|. Extra moves beyond M are either:
- internal moves (stay in block) that adjust k,
- additional block moves that cause backtracking (increase projected length).

- If last move into target H is from V below (N) -> k=0.
- From V above (S) -> k=K-1.
- From V left/right (E/W) -> k arbitrary.

Similarly source k matters for first move:
- If first move from source H is horizontal (E/W), source k arbitrary allowed. If first move vertical (N/S), source k must be boundary. If source k not boundary, need adjust before first move (cost maybe 2) or choose horizontal first if h>0.

Thus a general formula might be:
distance = 2*max(h,v) + source_penalty + target_penalty, where source_penalty/target_penalty are 0 or 2 depending on whether we can choose first/last move direction to accommodate k.

Let's test:
H-H h=v=1, source k=0, target k=K-1. 2m=2. Source: h>0 so can first move horizontal, source k=0 allowed, penalty0. Target: h>0 so can last move horizontal, target k arbitrary, penalty0. If first move horizontal, last move vertical; if first vertical, last horizontal. Thus cannot have both first and last horizontal. So source and target penalties interact: need choose order. If source k=0 allows horizontal first, then last is vertical forcing target k=0. If target wants K-1, need source maybe vertical first (requires k=K-1) and last horizontal. Source k=0 cannot vertical first. So no zero-penalty path; need extra.

This is like arranging a sequence of h horizontal and v vertical moves with no two same axis adjacent (for zero extra) and satisfying first/last constraints. If |h-v|≤1, the sequence is forced up to reversal: it must alternate. If h=v, there are two possible starting axes: H,V,H,V,... or V,H,V,H,... . If h=v+1, must start and end with horizontal. If v=h+1, must start and end with vertical. If h=v, one sequence starts with horizontal and ends with vertical; the other starts with vertical and ends with horizontal.

For H-H, source and target orientation H. The first move from H can be horizontal (E/W) if h>0, or vertical (N/S) if v>0. The last move into H can be horizontal if h>0, or vertical if v>0. The sequence axis pattern determines first/last axes.

Additionally, the specific direction (E vs W, N vs S) affects k forced values:
- H->V horizontal E sets V k=0; W sets V k=K-1.
- V->H vertical N sets H k=0; S sets H k=K-1.
- V->H horizontal E/W requires V k boundary and sets H arbitrary.
- H->V vertical N/S requires H k boundary and sets V arbitrary.

For H-H, if first move horizontal, source k arbitrary, but the V k after move is forced to 0 (E) or K-1 (W). This may affect subsequent moves. If first move vertical, source k must be 0 (S) or K-1 (N), and V k arbitrary can be chosen to satisfy next.

This is complex but manageable with a finite automaton over axis/direction and k boundary categories. Since h,v can be huge, but the optimal path likely uses an alternating sequence if possible; if |h-v|>1, extra moves add pairs to make sequence alternate. The cost might be 2*max(h,v) plus penalties. Let's derive general formula using "alternating path" model.

Observation: The cost of a path that uses only block moves and no internal moves, with no consecutive same axis, is exactly the number of block moves. The maximum number of horizontal moves h and vertical moves v in such a path with no consecutive same axis is constrained: |h-v| ≤ 1. If |h-v| > 1, to realize the required h and v without consecutive same axis, we need insert extra moves of the minority axis in opposite directions (backtracking) to separate the majority moves. The minimal number of block moves to have h net horizontal and v net vertical with no consecutive same axis is:
- If h >= v: need at least h horizontal moves. To avoid consecutive horizontal, between h horizontal moves there are h-1 gaps; each gap must contain at least one vertical move. Net vertical v can be achieved with at least v forward vertical moves plus extra pairs. Minimal total vertical moves = max(v, h-1) adjusted parity? Since extra vertical moves come in pairs (N/S) to keep net v. So total vertical moves V_total = v + 2*ceil((h-1 - v)/2) if h-1 > v, else v. Similarly total horizontal moves H_total = h. Total block moves = H_total + V_total. But each extra vertical pair adds 2 moves. Does this match H-H h=2,v=0: h-1=1 > v=0, V_total = 0 + 2*ceil(1/2)=2, total=4. h=4,v=0: h-1=3, V_total=2*ceil(3/2)=4, total=8. h=3,v=1: h-1=2 >1, V_total=1+2*ceil(1/2)=3, total=6 = 2*max? max=3 ->6. h=3,v=3: h-1=2 <=3, V_total=3, total6 = M. h=4,v=2: h-1=3 >2, V_total=2+2*ceil(1/2)=4, total8 = 2*max? max=4 ->8. h=4,v=4 total8. h=5,v=1: h-1=4 >1, V_total=1+2*ceil(3/2)=5, total10 = 2*max=10. h=5,v=3: h-1=4 >3, V_total=3+2*ceil(1/2)=5, total10 = 2*max. h=5,v=5 total10. So total block moves = 2*max(h,v) when h != v? Let's check h=4,v=2: 2*max=8 yes. h=4,v=0:8. h=3,v=1:6. h=3,v=3:6 =2*max=6. h=2,v=2:4. h=1,v=1:2. h=2,v=0:4. So indeed the minimal number of block moves with no consecutive same axis and net (h,v) is 2*max(h,v)! Because if h>=v, need h horizontal moves and at least h-1 vertical moves to separate, and total vertical moves must have same parity as v? Net vertical v, extra pairs, so V_total ≡ v mod 2. h and v have same parity for same orientation (H-H), so h-1 and v have opposite parity? If h,v same parity, h-1 has opposite parity. The minimal V_total ≥ max(v, h-1) with same parity as v is:
- If h-1 <= v: V_total = v (since v same parity as h, and h-1 opposite, so v > h-1). total h+v = 2*max? If h>=v and h-1<=v => h<=v+1. Since h>=v, h=v or v+1. But h,v same parity, so h=v. total2h=2max.
- If h-1 > v: minimal V_total = h-1 if h-1 ≡ v mod2, else h. Since h-1 and v opposite parity, so V_total = h. total h+h=2h=2max.
Thus total = 2*max(h,v) for same parity. Great! So the block-level cost to achieve net displacement with no consecutive same axis is 2*max(h,v), independent of k. This matches earlier 2*max formula for H-H/V-V. For different orientation, h+v is odd, so h,v have opposite parity. Let's compute minimal block moves no consecutive same axis:
If h>=v, h-1 and v? h and v opposite parity => h-1 same parity as v. Minimal V_total ≥ max(v, h-1) with parity v. If h-1 <= v (h<=v+1), V_total=v, total h+v = 2v+1? Since h=v+1 (opposite parity), total2v+1 = 2*max? max=h=v+1, 2max-1 =2v+1. If h-1 > v, V_total=h-1 (same parity), total h+h-1=2h-1 = 2max-1. So total = 2*max(h,v) - 1 for different orientation. Sample1 h=2,v=1: 2*2-1=3, but actual distance4 due to k penalties. So base block move count is 2*max - δ, where δ=1 if orientations differ? Let's check V-H sample1 base=3. H-V with h=1,v=0: V(0,1,K-1) to H(1,1,any) distance1. Base 2*1-1=1. Good. H-H base=2*max.

Thus the minimal number of block-changing moves (projection length) to go from source block to target block while avoiding consecutive same-axis (which is necessary to avoid hard passes) is:
B = 2*max(h,v) - (1 if orientations differ else 0).
This is exactly the length of an alternating path with no consecutive same axis. Let's verify H(0,0) to H(2,0): h=2,v=0 same orientation, B=4. H(0,0) to H(1,1): B=2. H(0,0) to H(0,2): B=4. Sample3 h=v=4e11 same, B=8e11. Sample2 h=2,v=2 B=4. Sample1 h=2,v=1 diff, B=3, but actual4 due to k. So distance ≥ B. Extra beyond B is due to source/target k constraints and maybe direction constraints along the alternating path.

Now, can every alternating path of length B be realized with appropriate k values? Not always; sample1 B=3 but distance4. The extra is 1, which is an internal move (not block move). So total distance = B + k_penalty, where k_penalty can be 0,1,2? Let's investigate.

Since B already avoids hard passes, the path through each block is a turn (enter from one axis, exit the other) or start/end. Turns are feasible if k constraints at the turn are satisfied. Let's analyze a turn at a block:
- H block: if path enters horizontally (from left/right V) and exits vertically (N/S), then entering H from long side gives arbitrary k; exiting vertical requires k=0 (S) or K-1 (N). So we can choose k on entry to match exit. Feasible.
  If path enters vertically and exits horizontally: entering vertical forces k=0 (from south) or K-1 (from north); exiting horizontal allowed from any k. Feasible.
  If path starts at H and exits horizontally: source k arbitrary, feasible.
  If path starts at H and exits vertically: source k must match boundary. If not, penalty.
  If path ends at H and entered horizontally: target k arbitrary, feasible.
  If path ends at H and entered vertically: target k forced to boundary. If target k doesn't match, penalty.
- V block analogous:
  - Turn between vertical (long) and horizontal (short): entering vertical gives arbitrary k, exiting horizontal requires k=0 (W) or K-1 (E); feasible by choosing k.
  - Entering horizontal and exiting vertical: entering horizontal forces k=0 (from west) or K-1 (from east); exiting vertical allowed; feasible.
  - Start V exit vertical: source k arbitrary, feasible.
  - Start V exit horizontal: source k must match boundary.
  - End V entered vertical: target k arbitrary? Wait entering V from H below/above (vertical move) is through long side, gives arbitrary k, so target k arbitrary. End V entered horizontal: entering from H left/right forces k=0/K-1, so target k must match.

Thus the only k constraints in an alternating path are at the start if the first move is in the "short direction" of the start tile, and at the end if the last move enters through the "short direction" of the target tile. All internal turns are feasible by choosing k appropriately. Is that true? Need check start V exit horizontal (short direction) requires source k=0/K-1; start H exit vertical requires source k=0/K-1. End H entered vertical (short direction) forces target k=0/K-1; end V entered horizontal forces target k=0/K-1. If the first move is in the long direction, source k arbitrary. If the last move enters through long direction, target k arbitrary. Internal turns always feasible because when entering through long side, you can choose k to satisfy exit short; when entering through short side, k is forced but exit long is allowed. What about two consecutive turns? Each block is a turn, so yes.

Therefore, for a given alternating path (sequence of axes), the only possible k penalty is at endpoints. If the required endpoint k doesn't match, we may need to insert an internal move at source or target (or choose a different alternating path with different first/last axis) to avoid penalty. Since B is fixed minimal block moves, extra k_penalty is the minimum cost to adjust endpoint k if needed. Internal adjustment at a tile from current k to required boundary costs min(|k - req|, 2?) Actually if source H needs to exit vertical requiring k=0 or K-1, and source k is not that, we can either:
- Before first move, move internally within source H to required k. Cost = |k - req| if direct, or via side V detour cost2. Since H_k to H_req via side V cost2 for any req, and direct cost |k-req|. Minimum = min(|k-req|, 2) for K>2; for K=2, min(|k-req|,1?) Actually if K=2, req is the other k, direct cost1, via side V2, so min=1. For K>2, if |k-req| =1, direct1; else2. So cost = min(|k-req|, 2) for K>2, and 1 for K=2 if different. But if source k already req, cost0.
Similarly target adjustment if last move enters through short side and target k forced to req, but target k differs: after entering target, move internally to target k. Cost = min(|target_k - req|, 2) (or 1 for K=2). However maybe we can choose a different alternating path with the other first/last axis to avoid penalty, at no extra block moves. Since B is minimal but there may be multiple alternating paths with different starting/ending axes.

Thus the problem reduces to:
1. Compute h = |ΔI|, v = |ΔJ|, and orientation difference δ = 0 if same (H-H or V-V), 1 if different.
2. B = 2*max(h,v) - δ.
3. Determine the set of possible first-axis and last-axis of alternating paths of length B with h horizontal and v vertical moves, no consecutive same axis, and net directions (E/W/N/S). For each combination, compute endpoint k requirement/penalty.
4. Minimal distance = B + min_penalty, where penalty is cost to adjust source/target k if needed. But wait if B=0 (same block), alternating path length 0; need handle separately.

Let's characterize alternating paths with no consecutive same axis and net h,v. Since all horizontal moves are in the same direction (E if ΔI>0, W if ΔI<0), and all vertical moves same direction (N if ΔJ>0, S if ΔJ<0). The sequence is an alternating sequence of H-moves and V-moves. The number of horizontal moves h, vertical v.

If h = v:
- Sequences can start with H or V.
  * Start H: H,V,H,V,...,V (length 2h, ends V)
  * Start V: V,H,V,H,...,H (ends H)
If h = v+1 (h > v):
- Must start and end with H: H,V,H,V,...,H (length 2h-1? Actually h=v+1, total 2v+1 = 2h-1 = B for same orientation? For same orientation h,v same parity, h=v+1 impossible because parity differs. So for same orientation h-v even, cannot be 1. For different orientation, h and v opposite parity, so |h-v| odd. Thus h=v+1 possible for diff orientation. B = 2h-1.)
If v = h+1:
- Must start and end with V.

For same orientation (h,v same parity):
- If h=v: both start possibilities.
- If |h-v| ≥ 2 even: the larger count > smaller+1, so to avoid consecutive same axis, we need extra moves of the smaller axis in both directions (backtracking), not just net moves. The alternating sequence of length B=2*max(h,v) will have the majority axis count = max, minority axis total = max (because B=2max, half each). Wait if h>v, B=2h, total vertical moves = h, but net vertical v < h. The extra vertical moves consist of forward and backward vertical moves. The sequence alternates H,V,H,V,... of length 2h, starting and ending with H. It has h horizontal moves and h vertical moves. Among the h vertical moves, the net vertical displacement is v, so there are (h+v)/2 forward and (h-v)/2 backward vertical moves. Thus the first and last axes are H (majority). Similarly if v>h, first/last V. So for same orientation with imbalance, the sequence is forced to start and end with the majority axis. If h=v, two possible starts.
For different orientation (h,v opposite parity):
- If |h-v|=1, B = h+v = 2*max-1. The sequence is perfectly alternating with no extra moves, starts and ends with the majority axis (if h=v+1 start/end H; if v=h+1 start/end V). If h=v? impossible due parity.
- If |h-v|≥3 odd, B=2*max-1. The majority axis count = max, minority total = max-1 (since B=2max-1). Sequence starts and ends with majority axis, with minority total max-1, net minority v < max-1? Wait if h>v, B=2h-1, total vertical moves = h-1. Net vertical v. Extra vertical pairs = (h-1 - v)/2. So first/last H. Similarly if v>h, first/last V.

Thus in all cases with B>0, the alternating path starts and ends with:
- If h > v: H-axis (horizontal moves) at both ends.
- If v > h: V-axis at both ends.
- If h == v: can start with either H or V, and ends with the other.

This is independent of orientation? Wait "H-axis" means a move in the horizontal direction (E/W), not tile orientation. Let's call axis X for horizontal block moves, Y for vertical block moves. The first/last axis is X if h>v, Y if v>h, either if h=v.

Now, the endpoint k constraints depend on the tile orientation (H or V) and the axis of the first/last move.

Let's define for a tile of orientation o:
- Long axis L: H tile long axis is X (horizontal); V tile long axis is Y (vertical).
- Short axis S: H tile short axis is Y; V tile short axis is X.

A move along the long axis (for start: exiting through long side; for end: entering through long side) imposes no k constraint.
A move along the short axis imposes:
- Start exiting short:
  * H tile: exit Y. Direction N requires k=K-1; S requires k=0.
  * V tile: exit X. Direction E requires k=K-1; W requires k=0.
- End entering short:
  * H tile: enter Y. From N (V above -> H south? Need coordinate: last move into H from north means previous block is (I,J+1) V, move S. Enter H through top side, forces k=K-1. From south (previous V below, move N), enter bottom, forces k=0.)
    Let's define last move direction d (from previous block to target):
      If d = N (moving north into target), target is above previous. For H target, previous V below, entering through bottom side -> k=0.
      If d = S (moving south into target), previous V above, entering through top side -> k=K-1.
      If d = E (moving east into target), previous V left, entering through left side (long) -> arbitrary.
      If d = W, previous V right, entering through right side (long) -> arbitrary.
  * V target: long axis Y. Entering through Y (d=N from H below? Wait moving N into V target from H below: enter bottom side, which is long -> arbitrary. d=S from H above: enter top side long -> arbitrary. d=E from H left: enter left side short -> k=0? Let's check V target at (I,J), previous H left (I-1,J), move E. V_0 left edge, k=0. d=W from H right, V_{K-1} right edge, k=K-1.)
So short-axis entry for V is X (E/W), forced k=0 for E, K-1 for W.

Start exiting short:
  * H: d=N requires k=K-1; d=S requires k=0.
  * V: d=E requires k=K-1; d=W requires k=0.

Thus the required k at an endpoint is determined by the direction of the first/last move if that move is along the tile's short axis. If along long axis, no requirement.

Now, in an alternating path with first axis X or Y, the actual first direction is determined by sign of ΔI/ΔJ:
- X moves: if ΔI > 0, direction E; if ΔI < 0, W. (h>0)
- Y moves: if ΔJ > 0, direction N; if ΔJ < 0, S. (v>0)
If h=0, no X moves; if v=0, no Y moves. The first/last axis may be X only if h>0, Y only if v>0.

If h==v and both >0, there are two possible alternating paths:
- Start X, end Y.
- Start Y, end X.
If h>v, start/end X. If v>h, start/end Y.
If h=0 or v=0, the majority axis is the only axis; B=2*max - δ. For same orientation with v=0, h even >0, start/end X. But a path with only X moves would have consecutive X moves, so B includes extra Y moves (backtracking) to alternate. The first/last axis is X, yes. Now, for a given start axis A and end axis B (each X or Y), we can compute whether endpoint k constraints are avoidable:
- Start: if A == long_axis(source orientation), no source penalty. If A == short_axis, source must have k = req_start(direction). If source k matches, penalty0; else need adjust source k to req before first move, cost c_adj(source_k, req).
- End: if B == long_axis(target orientation), no target penalty. If B == short_axis, target must have k = req_end(direction). If target k matches, penalty0; else need adjust target k after last move, cost c_adj(target_k, req).

But wait if start axis is short and source k doesn't match, we can adjust source k before first move. This adds internal moves, increasing distance. Similarly target. The adjustment cost is min(|k - req|, 2) for K>2, or 1 for K=2 if different. Let's define c_adj(k, req):
- If k == req: 0.
- If K == 2: req is the other k (0 or1), cost 1.
- Else: min(abs(k-req), 2). Since req is 0 or K-1. If k is 0 and req K-1, abs=K-1, min=2 (unless K=2). If k=1, req0 abs1 cost1; reqK-1 absK-2 cost2 for K>3? If K=3, abs1 cost1. However, if both source and target need adjustment, could we instead choose a path with different start/end axis to avoid one or both? We take min over possible axis pairs.

This is getting detailed but manageable. Since T up to 1e4, we can implement a search over possible start/end axes and directions? The number of possibilities is small. We can enumerate the first move direction and last move direction among the four directions, and check if there exists an alternating path with net (h,v), no consecutive same axis, total length B, starting with that direction and ending with that direction. Then compute endpoint penalties. Since directions are only 4, we can brute force feasibility combinatorially.

But maybe there is an even simpler way: The minimal distance might be computed by considering the source and target "ports" and using a formula with B plus endpoint penalties where endpoint penalties are determined by whether the first/last move can be chosen along the tile's long axis. We can enumerate the first axis (X/Y) and direction, last axis/direction, and check if an alternating sequence with those endpoints exists. Let's derive feasibility conditions for an alternating sequence with:
- Total length B (known).
- Net horizontal h (signed? We'll use absolute and direction sign; but for endpoint direction we need actual E/W/N/S. Let's define h_abs, v_abs, and signs sh = sign(ΔI) (0 if h=0), sv = sign(ΔJ).
- Total X moves Xtot, Ytot Ytot as above.
- Start direction d1 (one of E/W/N/S or None if B=0), end direction dL.
- Sequence alternates axes, no consecutive same axis.
- Net X = sh * h_abs, net Y = sv * v_abs.

We can check existence by counting.

Let's define for each axis:
- X moves: total nX. Let e = number of E moves, w = number of W moves. e + w = nX, e - w = sh * h_abs (could be 0). Thus e = (nX + sh*h)/2, w = (nX - sh*h)/2. Need integers >=0.
- Y moves: total nY. n = number of N, s = number of S. n + s = nY, n - s = sv*v. n = (nY + sv*v)/2, s = (nY - sv*v)/2.

Given nX,nY determined by orientation/imbalance, these counts are fixed. The only freedom is ordering the multiset of moves (with counts e,w,n,s) into an alternating sequence starting with d1, ending with dL, no adjacent same axis.

This is a combinatorial existence problem. Since counts can be huge, but we can derive conditions.

An alternating sequence of X/Y moves is determined by the starting axis and the sequence of axes. The counts of X and Y moves and start/end axes determine how many X-Y adjacencies. The directions within each axis can be arranged arbitrarily among the X positions (e E's and w W's) and Y positions (n N's and s S's), with the only constraints on the first and last positions if they are of that axis.

Thus, for a given start axis and end axis (which determine if d1 is X/Y and dL is X/Y), we just need:
- If start axis is X, choose d1 ∈ {E,W} with available count >0 (if nX>0). If start axis Y, choose d1 ∈ {N,S} with count >0.
- If end axis is X, choose dL ∈ {E,W} with available count >0 (if nX>0), and if start also X and nX=1, the same move is both first and last; need d1=dL and count at least1. More generally, if start and end are same axis and nX=1, then the single X move is both first and last, so d1=dL. If nX>1, first and last X positions are distinct, so counts must allow both.
- Similarly for Y.

Additionally, if start axis and end axis are different, the first and last positions are different axes, so no conflict.

Also need nX,nY >0 if axis appears. If h=0 but nX>0 due to extra opposite X moves, X axis appears. If h=0 and v>0, nX=M >0 for same orientation? For same orientation h=0,v=2, M=2, nX=2, yes X appears. If h=0,v=0 (same block), B=0 special.

Let's compute nX,nY:
Let M = max(h_abs, v_abs).
If orientation same (δ=0):
  B = 2M, nX = M, nY = M.
If orientation different (δ=1):
  B = 2M - 1.
  If h_abs > v_abs: nX = M, nY = M-1.
  If v_abs > h_abs: nX = M-1, nY = M.
  (h_abs != v_abs because parity diff)

Check if h_abs=0,v_abs=1 (different orientation): M=1, v>h, nX=0,nY=1. B=1. Sequence just Y. Good.

Now, possible start/end axes for an alternating sequence with these counts:
- If nX == nY: start can be X (end Y) or Y (end X).
- If nX == nY + 1: start=end=X.
- If nY == nX + 1: start=end=Y.
This is forced by counts. So we can enumerate the 1 or 2 axis patterns.

For each axis pattern, we can enumerate possible d1/dL directions from available counts and compute endpoint penalties. Since only 4 directions, we can brute force.

But wait: Does the axis pattern always start with the majority count? For nX=nY, both start options. For nX=nY+1, start/end X. Yes.

Now, endpoint penalty:
- Source tile orientation o_s, target o_t.
- If first move direction d1 exists, it determines whether source exits through long or short side. We can compute required source k if short:
  * Source H (o=0):
    - d1 = N: req = K-1
    - d1 = S: req = 0
    - d1 = E/W: no req (long)
  * Source V (o=1):
    - d1 = E: req = K-1
    - d1 = W: req = 0
    - d1 = N/S: no req (long)
- If no first move (B=0), no source penalty.
- Last move direction dL into target:
  * Target H:
    - dL = N: entering from south, req = 0
    - dL = S: entering from north, req = K-1
    - dL = E/W: no req (long)
  * Target V:
    - dL = E: entering from west, req = 0
    - dL = W: entering from east, req = K-1
    - dL = N/S: no req (long)

Wait for target H, dL=N means previous block is south, move north into target. H bottom edge, k=0. Yes.
For target V, dL=E means previous block west, V_0 left edge, k=0. dL=W means previous east, V_{K-1} right edge, k=K-1.

Then source_penalty = c_adj(s_k, req) if req exists else 0.
Target_penalty = c_adj(t_k, req) if req exists else 0.
Total penalty = source_penalty + target_penalty.

But is it always valid to adjust source/target k independently without affecting the alternating path? If we adjust source k before first move, we add internal moves. This changes the starting tile's k to req. The first move then feasible. Similarly target adjustment after last move. These additions don't affect block sequence. So yes.

However, if source and target are the same tile (B=0), distance is 0, not adjustment. Need handle.

Also, if source and target are in same block but different k (same orientation), B=0? h=0,v=0, orientation same. Our formula B=0, axis pattern none. Distance should be min(|k_s-k_t|, 2) (or 1 for K=2) as earlier. But can this be represented as B + endpoint penalties? No, because no block moves. We'll handle same block separately.

If source and target in same block but same orientation and different k, distance = min(|k_s-k_t|, 2) for K>2? Actually via side V cost2, direct |diff|. For K=2, min(|diff|,2)=1. So formula min(abs(diff), 2) works for all K>=2? If K=2, abs=1, min=1. If K>2, if diff=1 cost1, else2. Yes. So same block distance = min(abs(k_s-k_t), 2). But wait if K=2 and diff=1, direct internal cost1; min=1. Good.

What if source and target in same block but different orientation? Impossible because block orientation fixed.

Now, let's test our model on sample1.

Sample1: K=3, S V(-1,0,k=1), T H(1,-1,k=2).
Compute block coords:
S I=-1,J=0, o=V (1), k=1.
T I=1,J=-1, o=H (0), k=2.
h_abs = |1-(-1)|=2, v_abs = |-1-0|=1. ΔI=+2 (E), ΔJ=-1 (S). Orientations differ δ=1. M=2. h>v, so nX=M=2, nY=M-1=1. B=2M-1=3. Axis pattern: nX=nY+1 -> start=end=X. So first move must be X (E/W), last move X. nX=2, nY=1. Net X=+2, so e=2,w=0. Net Y=-1, nY=1, so s=1,n=0. Sequence axes: X,Y,X (since start/end X). Directions: X moves both E; Y move S. So d1=E, dL=E.

Source V, d1=E. For V, short axis X, E requires req=K-1=2. Source k=1, c_adj=min(|1-2|,2)=1. Source penalty1.
Target H, dL=E. For H, long axis X, no req. Target penalty0.
Total distance = B +1 =4. Matches sample1.

Sample2: K=4, S H(2,2,k=0), T H(0,0,k=2).
h_abs=2, v_abs=2, same orientation δ=0, M=2, B=4, nX=2,nY=2. Axis patterns: start X end Y, or start Y end X.
Net X=-2 (W), e=0,w=2. Net Y=-2 (S), n=0,s=2.

Pattern start Y end X: d1=N, dL=E.
Source H d1=N short req=K-1=4, source k=0 penalty2; target no. Min B. Matches.

Great.

Now test H(0,0,k=0) to H(1,1,k=K-1), K=3.
h=1,v=1 same, B=2, nX=1,nY=1. Net E,N.
Pattern start X end Y: d1=E, dL=N. Source no, target H dL=N req=0, target k=2 c_adj=min(2,2)=2 -> distance4.
Pattern start Y end X: d1=N, dL=E. Source H d1=N req=K-1=2, source k=0 c_adj=2, target no -> distance4. Min4. Matches.

Test H(0,0,k=0) to H(0,2,k=K-1), K=3.
h=0,v=2 same, M=2, B=4, nX=2,nY=2. Net X=0, Y=+2. Counts: e=1,w=1; n=2,s=0.
Axis patterns:
1 start X end Y. Need sequence X,Y,X,Y. d1 can be E or W? Since e=1,w=1, both available. dL is Y, must be N (only N). Let's enumerate possible d1:
- d1=E, dL=N. Source H d1=E long no req. Target H dL=N req=0, target k=2 c_adj=2 -> total B+2=6.
- d1=W, dL=N. Source H d1=W long no req. Target req0 penalty2 ->6.
Pattern start Y end X: d1=N, dL can be E or W.

So min6. Matches our found path. Good.

Test H(0,0,k=0) to H(2,0,k=0), K=3.
h=2,v=0 same, B=4, nX=2,nY=2. Net X=+2, Y=0. Counts e=2,w=0; n=1,s=1.
Axis patterns:

- d1=E source no. dL=N target H req=0, target k=0 penalty0 -> distance4.
- dL=S target req=K-1=2, target k=0 c_adj=2 ->6.

- d1=S source req=0, source k=0 penalty0; dL=E target no ->4.

Test H(0,0,k=0) to H(2,0,k=K-1): pattern start X end Y dL=N gives target req0 penalty2 ->6; dL=S req2 target k=2 penalty0 ->4. So distance4. Good.

Test V(0,1,k=0) to H(1,1,k=K-1) (V-H diff, h=1,v=0? Actually V(0,1) to H(1,1): ΔI=1,ΔJ=0, diff orientation, M=1, h>v, nX=1,nY=0, B=1, start=end X, d1=E. Source V d1=E req=K-1. If source k=0, c_adj=2, distance3. Is that correct? V(0,1,0) to H(1,1,K-1): V E requires k=K-1; can adjust V k to K-1 via H above cost2, then E cost1 total3. If source k=K-1, distance1. Good.

Test V(0,1,k=K-1) to H(1,1,k=0): source no, target H dL=E long no req, distance1. V E to H(1,1,any) yes.

Now, need ensure that the alternating path with total counts nX,nY and net counts is always realizable for the chosen start/end directions. We need implement a feasibility check.

Given:
- nX, nY counts.
- net counts e,w,n,s as above.
- start axis ax1 (X/Y), end axis axL.
- start direction d1 (if ax1), end direction dL (if axL).

We need know if there exists an alternating sequence of length B starting with d1 and ending with dL, with the given multiset of directions and axis counts.

Since axes alternate, the axis sequence is uniquely determined by start axis and counts? Actually if nX=nY, start X gives axis sequence X,Y,X,Y,...,Y (length even). If start Y gives Y,X,...,X. If nX=nY+1, start=end=X gives X,Y,X,Y,...,X. If nY=nX+1, start=end=Y gives Y,X,Y,...,Y. So axis sequence is fixed (up to reversal? But start/end fixed). Thus the positions of X moves and Y moves are fixed. We just need to assign the multiset of directions to X positions and Y positions such that the first X position gets d1 if start axis X, the last X position gets dL if end axis X, and similarly for Y. This is possible iff:
- For each axis A, if it appears:
  * If the first position of the sequence is A, then the direction assigned to that position must be d1. So the count of d1 among that axis's directions must be ≥1.
  * If the last position is A, then direction assigned to last position must be dL. Count ≥1.
  * If the first and last positions are both A and there is only one position of A (nA=1), then that single position is both first and last, so d1 must equal dL and count of that direction ≥1. If nA>1, first and last positions are distinct, so we need count(d1)≥1 and count(dL)≥1; if d1==dL, need count≥2.
  * For axes not at endpoints, no direction constraints.
Since all other positions can be filled arbitrarily with remaining counts, the above conditions are sufficient (counts nonnegative).

But wait, if start axis X and end axis Y, the first position is X, last is Y. We need count of d1 among X ≥1, count of dL among Y ≥1. No conflict. If start=end=X, first and last X positions: if nX=1, d1=dL and count≥1; if nX>1, need count(d1)≥1 and count(dL)≥1 (if same, count≥2). Similarly for Y if start=end=Y.

Also, if d1/dL direction belongs to the wrong axis? We only choose d1 from start axis directions, dL from end axis directions.

Now, what about the case B=0 (same block): handle separately.

What about source/target orientation and B? We used δ = 0 if orientations same else 1. But orientation is determined by (I+J)&1. If source and target blocks maybe same orientation but h,v parity? For same orientation, h+v even, so h,v same parity. Our nX=nY=M works. If different orientation, h+v odd, h,v opposite parity, nX/nY as above. Need ensure counts e,w,n,s are integers:
e = (nX + sh*h)/2. Since nX = M or M-1. Need nX ≡ h mod2. We argued yes. But if h=0, sh=0, e=w=nX/2. nX may be M. For same orientation h=0,v=2, M=2, nX=2, e=1,w=1. Good. For different orientation h=0,v=1, M=1, v>h, nX=M-1=0, e=w=0. Good. If h=0,v=3 diff? h+v odd? 0+3 odd, M=3, v>h, nX=2, e=w=1. Good.

Now, we should verify the base B formula with actual distance for a case where source/target k require no penalty and B is large. Sample3 yes. Let's test a random small case by manual BFS? Maybe we can write mental for K=3, H(0,0,k=0) to V(1,0,k=0) (adjacent east). h=1,v=0, diff orientation, M=1, h>v, nX=1,nY=0, B=1, start=end X, d1=E. Source H d1=E long no req. Target V dL=E (entering from west) short req=0. Target k=0 penalty0. Distance1. Correct.

H(0,0,k=0) to V(1,0,k=K-1): same B=1, target V dL=E req=0, target k=K-1 c_adj=2 -> distance3. Is there a 1-move path? H E to V(1,0,0) only, not K-1. Need adjust target after entering: V(1,0,0) to V(1,0,K-1) cost2 via H above, total3. Or before? H(0,0,0) to V(1,0,0) cost1, V internal to K-1 cost2, total3. Good.

H(0,0,k=0) to V(1,1,k=0) (h=1,v=1 diff? H(0,0) parity even, V(1,1) parity even? 1+1=2 even, wait V orientation requires odd parity. (1,1) sum even -> H, not V. Need choose target V with parity odd, e.g., V(1,0) h=1,v=0; V(0,1) h=0,v=1; V(1,2) h=1,v=2 diff? h=1,v=2, M=2, v>h, nX=1,nY=2, B=3. Let's test H(0,0,k=0) to V(1,2,k=0). Block path maybe N,E,N? H(0,0) N requires k=K-1, source k=0 penalty2. Sequence start/end Y (v>h): d1=N, dL=N? Wait nY=2,nX=1, start=end Y, axis sequence Y,X,Y. Net Y=+2 (two N), X=+1 (E). d1=N, dL=N. Source H d1=N req=K-1, source k=0 penalty2. Target V dL=N (entering from south? Last move N into V from H below, long side) no req. Distance B+2=5. Is there shorter? Maybe H(0,0,0) E to V(1,0,0), V N to H(1,1,0), H N? H(1,1,0) N not allowed (k=0), need adjust. Could be 5. Seems plausible.

Now, we need be careful: The alternating path with no consecutive same axis is sufficient to avoid hard passes, but is it always feasible internally? We assumed turns are feasible by choosing k on entry. Let's prove:
- At an H block, if path enters from left/right (long) and exits top/bottom (short), entering long gives arbitrary k; choose k to match exit requirement. If enters from top/bottom (short) and exits left/right (long), entering short forces k=0/K-1; exiting long allowed from any k. If starts at H and exits long, source k arbitrary; if exits short, source k must match (we handle penalty). If ends at H entered long, target k arbitrary; entered short, target k forced (we handle penalty). Similar V. Thus yes.

But what about a path that goes through a block with two consecutive moves of the same axis but different directions (e.g., E then W) as part of an alternating sequence? That would be same axis adjacent, forbidden. Our alternating sequence avoids any adjacent same axis. Extra opposite moves are separated by other axis moves. Good.

Now, is the minimal block move count always B = 2M - δ? We derived by counts. Let's prove:
To avoid hard passes, no two consecutive moves can have the same axis. Thus the sequence of block moves must alternate axes. Therefore the total number of X moves and Y moves differ by at most 1. Let Xtot,Ytot. Net horizontal h, vertical v. We need Xtot ≥ h, Xtot ≡ h mod2; Ytot ≥ v, Ytot ≡ v mod2; |Xtot-Ytot|≤1. Minimize B=Xtot+Ytot. Let M=max(h,v). Since Xtot+Ytot ≥ h+v, but also because counts differ by ≤1, B is at least 2M-1? Let's solve:
If h>=v. Need Xtot ≥ h. Since Ytot ≥ Xtot-1 (because differ ≤1 and Xtot maybe larger), B ≥ 2Xtot-1 ≥ 2h-1. Also parity constraints. If h,v same parity (same orientation), B even, so B ≥ 2h (since 2h-1 odd). Achievable with Xtot=Ytot=h. If h,v opposite parity, B odd, so B ≥ 2h-1, achievable with Xtot=h,Ytot=h-1. Thus B = 2M - δ where δ=1 if opposite parity (different orientation), else 0. Good.

Now, endpoint penalty c_adj = min(abs(k-req), 2). Is it always possible to adjust k by 2 using two moves without affecting block sequence? For source, we can insert internal moves before first block move. But if source k differs from req by 2, direct internal cost 2, or via side V cost2. If by 1, cost1. If by >2, cost2 via side V. So c_adj works. For target, after last block move, adjust similarly. However, if source and target are the same tile, B=0 and our endpoint penalties would double count; handle separately.

But wait: If source adjustment uses a side V detour, that detour consists of block moves (e.g., H->V->H) that change the block coordinates and might affect the net displacement? They are backtracking and would be extra block moves beyond B. Our c_adj=2 represents those two block moves, not internal moves. But in our distance formula, we add c_adj to B. That's fine; the total block moves become B + (number of detour moves if used). If c_adj=1, it's an internal move; if c_adj=2, it could be either two internal moves (if direct k diff=2) or two block moves (detour). Both add 2 to distance. So adding c_adj works.

But is it always valid to perform source adjustment before the first block move if the first block move is along the short axis and requires req? Yes, after adjustment source k=req, then first move feasible. If adjustment uses a detour, it changes source tile to another tile in same block with req k, so still in source block. Good.

Now, what if both source and target penalties are 2, and they could be combined by choosing a different path? Our enumeration over axis patterns and directions already minimizes over possible first/last directions. It might still overestimate if source and target are the same block? handled. If source and target are different blocks but B maybe 1, and both source and target require adjustment, could there be a path that adjusts k in the intermediate tile instead, costing less? Example V(0,1,k=0) to H(1,1,k=K-1), B=1, source req K-1 penalty2, target no (long). Distance3. If target k=0, distance1. If source k=0,target k=0, distance1. Good.
What about H(0,0,k=0) to V(1,0,k=K-1), B=1, source no, target req0 penalty2, distance3. Could instead move H->V(1,0,0), then adjust V to K-1 cost2, yes.

Now, let's test a case where B>0 and both source and target penalties maybe 2, but a different axis pattern reduces to 0. Our enumeration handles.

Let's implement the algorithm:

For each test case:
1. Compute block coordinates:
   I_s = floor_div(Sx, K) = Sx // K in Python (floor for negative).
   J_s = Sy // K.
   I_t = Tx // K.
   J_t = Ty // K.
   Need ensure floor((Sx+0.5)/K) = Sx//K for negative? We checked. Python // floors. Good.
2. Determine orientation:
   o_s = (I_s + J_s) & 1. If 0 -> H, 1 -> V.
   o_t = (I_t + J_t) & 1.
3. Determine k:
   If o_s == 0 (H): k_s = Sy - J_s * K. Since Sy = J_s*K + offset, offset 0..K-1. For negative, Sy//K floor, offset nonnegative. Example Sy=-1,K=3: J=-1, offset = -1 - (-3)=2. Good.
   Else (V): k_s = Sx - I_s * K.
   Similarly target:
   If o_t == 0: k_t = Ty - J_t * K.
   Else: k_t = Tx - I_t * K.
4. If I_s == I_t and J_s == J_t:
   Since same block, orientation same. Distance = min(abs(k_s - k_t), 2). Is this always? What if source and target are same tile (k equal): 0. If K=2 and diff1:1. If K>2 diff maybe >2: via side V cost2. But wait if the block is H, H_k to H_k' via side V cost2 for any k,k'. If K=2, cost1. min(abs,2) gives1. If K>2 and diff=1, cost1; diff=2, direct2 or via side2; min2. Good. But is there any case where same block but source/target orientation? Same block orientation fixed. So answer = min(abs(k_s-k_t), 2). Let's verify for K=2, H(0,0,0) to H(0,0,1): adjacent internal, cost1. min(1,2)=1. Good.
   However, what if same block but source and target are the same tile? min(0,2)=0. Good.
5. Else:
   h = abs(I_t - I_s)
   v = abs(J_t - J_s)
   sh = 1 if I_t > I_s else -1 if I_t < I_s else 0
   sv = 1 if J_t > J_s else -1 if J_t < J_s else 0
   M = max(h,v)
   if o_s == o_t: delta = 0 else 1
   B = 2*M - delta
   # Determine nX, nY
   if delta == 0:
       nX = M
       nY = M
   else:
       if h > v:
           nX = M
           nY = M - 1
       elif v > h:
           nX = M - 1
           nY = M
       else:
           # Should not happen because diff orientation => h+v odd, h!=v
           # but if h=v=0 same block handled.
           pass
   # Counts of directions:
   # e = (nX + sh*h)//2, w = (nX - sh*h)//2
   # n = (nY + sv*v)//2, s = (nY - sv*v)//2
   # Need ensure nonnegative integers.
   e = (nX + sh*h)//2
   w = (nX - sh*h)//2
   nn = (nY + sv*v)//2
   ss = (nY - sv*v)//2
   # Build axis patterns:
   patterns = []
   if nX == nY:
       patterns.append(('X','Y'))
       patterns.append(('Y','X'))
   elif nX == nY + 1:
       patterns.append(('X','X'))
   elif nY == nX + 1:
       patterns.append(('Y','Y'))
   # For each pattern, enumerate possible d1/dL:
   best_penalty = large
   for ax1, axL in patterns:
       # possible directions for start axis:
       if ax1 == 'X':
           start_dirs = []
           if e > 0: start_dirs.append('E')
           if w > 0: start_dirs.append('W')
       else:
           start_dirs = []
           if nn > 0: start_dirs.append('N')
           if ss > 0: start_dirs.append('S')
       # end dirs similarly for axL
       if axL == 'X':
           end_dirs = []
           if e > 0: end_dirs.append('E')
           if w > 0: end_dirs.append('W')
       else:
           end_dirs = []
           if nn > 0: end_dirs.append('N')
           if ss > 0: end_dirs.append('S')
       # But need handle if start and end same axis and nA=1 conflict.
       # We'll enumerate d1 in start_dirs, dL in end_dirs and check counts feasibility.
       for d1 in start_dirs:
           for dL in end_dirs:
               if feasible_directions(ax1, axL, d1, dL, e,w,nn,ss):
                   pen = source_penalty(o_s, k_s, d1) + target_penalty(o_t, k_t, dL)
                   if pen < best_penalty: best_penalty = pen
   ans = B + best_penalty
   # But what if no pattern? Shouldn't happen.
   print(ans)

Need implement feasible_directions.

Given axis sequence fixed by ax1 and counts. We need check if we can assign directions to positions.

Let's define counts dict for X: {'E':e,'W':w}; for Y: {'N':nn,'S':ss}.

We know first position axis = ax1, last = axL.

If ax1 == 'X':
   # The first X position must get d1.
   # If axL == 'X' and nX == 1, then first=last same X position, so d1 must equal dL and count[d1]>=1.
   # Else need count[d1]>=1.
   # If axL == 'X' and nX > 1, need count[dL]>=1 additionally; if d1==dL need count>=2.
   # If axL != 'X', no end X constraint.
Similarly if ax1 == 'Y':
   # first Y position gets d1.
   # If axL == 'Y' and nY == 1, d1==dL and count>=1.
   # If axL == 'Y' and nY > 1, need count[dL]>=1 (and if same count>=2).
   # If axL != 'Y', no end Y constraint.
But also if axL == 'X' and ax1 != 'X', then the last X position must get dL; need count[dL]>=1. If axL == 'Y' and ax1 != 'Y', need count[dL]>=1.
Actually the above can be unified:
- If first axis is X, require count_X[d1] >= 1.
- If first axis is Y, require count_Y[d1] >= 1.
- If last axis is X:
   - If first axis is also X and nX == 1: require d1 == dL and count_X[d1] >= 1 (already).
   - Else require count_X[dL] >= 1, and if first axis X and d1==dL and nX >1? Wait if first and last X positions are distinct, requiring count_X[d1]>=1 and count_X[dL]>=1 is enough. If d1==dL, count_X[d1]>=1 satisfies both because the same direction can be used in two distinct positions only if count>=2. But if count=1, can we use the same direction for both first and last X positions? They are distinct positions, so need two moves of that direction. Thus need count_X[d1] >= 2 if d1==dL and nX>1. If d1!=dL, need count_X[d1]>=1 and count_X[dL]>=1.
- Similarly for Y.
So implement:

def feasible(ax1, axL, d1, dL, e,w,nn,ss):
    cntX = {'E':e,'W':w}
    cntY = {'N':nn,'S':ss}
    # first constraint
    if ax1 == 'X':
        if cntX[d1] < 1: return False
    else:
        if cntY[d1] < 1: return False
    # last constraint
    if axL == 'X':
        if ax1 == 'X' and nX == 1:
            # single X is both first and last
            if d1 != dL: return False
            if cntX[d1] < 1: return False
        else:
            if cntX[dL] < 1: return False
            if ax1 == 'X' and d1 == dL and nX > 1 and cntX[d1] < 2:
                return False
    else: # axL == 'Y'
        if ax1 == 'Y' and nY == 1:
            if d1 != dL: return False
            if cntY[d1] < 1: return False
        else:
            if cntY[dL] < 1: return False
            if ax1 == 'Y' and d1 == dL and nY > 1 and cntY[d1] < 2:
                return False
    return True

But wait, what if ax1 != axL and nX=0 or nY=0? Then axis pattern wouldn't have that axis at endpoint. If nX=0, ax1/axL cannot be X. Our patterns ensure.

Also, if nX==nY and pattern start X end Y, nX,nY >0 unless M=0 (handled). Good.

Now, source_penalty and target_penalty functions:

def source_penalty(o, k, d):
    if o == 0: # H
        if d == 'N':
            req = K - 1
        elif d == 'S':
            req = 0
        else:
            return 0
    else: # V
        if d == 'E':
            req = K - 1
        elif d == 'W':
            req = 0
        else:
            return 0
    return min(abs(k - req), 2)

def target_penalty(o, k, d):
    if o == 0: # H
        if d == 'N':
            req = 0
        elif d == 'S':
            req = K - 1
        else:
            return 0
    else: # V
        if d == 'E':
            req = 0
        elif d == 'W':
            req = K - 1
        else:
            return 0
    return min(abs(k - req), 2)

Now, let's think about the proof that the minimal distance is B + min endpoint penalty. Is there any scenario where an internal turn in the alternating path requires k adjustment costing more than 0? We argued turns are feasible by choosing k on entry. But let's double-check a turn at a V block: path enters V from H below (vertical move N) and exits V to H right (horizontal move E). Entering V from below is through long side (bottom), so k can be chosen arbitrarily. Exiting V to H right requires V k=K-1. Choose k=K-1 on entry. Feasible. Path enters V from H left (horizontal E) and exits V to H above (vertical N). Entering V from left is short side, forces k=0. Exiting V to H above is long side, allowed from any k. Feasible. What if path enters V from H left (k=0) and exits V to H right (horizontal E)? That would be two horizontal moves through V, which is a hard pass; our alternating sequence avoids adjacent same axis, so this doesn't occur. Good.

What about a path that starts at H and exits horizontal (long) with source k arbitrary, then later enters a V block horizontally? The first move H->V horizontal sets V k=0 or K-1. The next move from V must be vertical (alternating), which is long side exit, allowed from any k. So fine.

Thus endpoint-only penalties suffice.

Now, we should consider if the alternating sequence with extra opposite moves (backtracking) might cause the path to revisit blocks and possibly create cycles that allow even shorter distance than B + penalties? B is the minimal block move count avoiding hard passes. Any path with hard passes would require internal adjustment inside the block, which costs at least 1 or 2. Could a path with fewer block moves but more internal moves be shorter than B + endpoint penalties? For example, H-H h=2,v=0, B=4. But detour adjustment inside V via H above would add 2 block moves, total block moves 4 = B. So hard pass with detour is exactly an alternating path with extra block moves. Maybe for K=2, distance can be computed by considering a path that is monotone in block coordinates (no backtracking) with possible hard passes. Since hard passes cost 1.5 per block? Let's derive.

A monotone path from (I,J) to (I',J') consists of h horizontal moves and v vertical moves in some order. Each time we move horizontally, we alternate between H and V blocks. A horizontal run of length r (consecutive E moves) passes through r-1 V blocks? Actually if you make r consecutive E moves, you visit r+1 blocks. The number of V blocks traversed horizontally (where you enter a V block from west and exit east) is floor(r/2)? Let's examine start orientation matters.

For K=2, cost of a horizontal run of length r:
- If starting block orientation is H (so first E enters V), then after 1 E you're in V. A second E exits V to H. So each pair of E moves (H->V->H) costs 3 (2 block moves +1 internal in V). If r is even, cost = r + r/2 = 1.5r. If r is odd, starting H, after odd E you end in V; no exit from last V. The internal flips occur for each V block fully traversed, which is floor(r/2). Cost = r + floor(r/2). Similarly vertical runs through H blocks cost same.

Thus the total cost of a monotone path is h + v + (number of fully traversed V blocks horizontally) + (number of fully traversed H blocks vertically). A fully traversed V block horizontally occurs when two consecutive horizontal moves cross a V block; i.e., for each adjacent pair of horizontal moves in the sequence? If horizontal moves are consecutive, pairs within runs. The number of such V blocks is the number of times the path goes from H to V to H horizontally, which equals the number of adjacent horizontal pairs in the move sequence? More precisely, if horizontal moves are grouped into runs, each run of length r contributes floor(r/2) V-block traversals. Similarly vertical runs contribute floor(r/2) H-block traversals.

To minimize cost, we can choose the order of horizontal and vertical moves to minimize the number of adjacent same-axis pairs (hard passes) because each hard pass costs +1 internal. But for K=2, a hard pass costs +1, while an extra turn (inserting opposite move) costs +2 block moves. So we want to minimize number of hard passes, but not necessarily eliminate them. The minimal number of hard passes needed in a sequence with h horizontal and v vertical moves is:
- If we arrange moves to alternate as much as possible, the number of adjacent same-axis pairs is max(0, h - v - 1?) Let's compute.

A sequence of h X moves and v Y moves. The number of adjacent pairs of same axis is minimized by alternating. The minimum number of same-axis adjacencies is:
If h >= v: minimum same-X adjacencies = h - v - 1 if h > v? Let's derive. In an alternating sequence with no same adjacencies, counts differ by at most1. If h > v, there will be h - v - 1 extra X moves that must be adjacent to X, creating that many same-X adjacencies? Example h=4,v=0: sequence X X X X has 3 same-X adjacencies. Formula h-v-1=3. h=4,v=2: can sequence X Y X Y X X? Same-X adjacencies? X Y X Y X X has one XX at end. h-v-1=1. h=3,v=1: X Y X X has one XX, formula1. h=2,v=0: one XX, formula1. h=2,v=2:0. Each same-axis adjacency corresponds to a hard pass through a block of the opposite orientation? For horizontal same adjacency (XX), the intermediate block is V, and it's a horizontal pass through V. Cost +1 internal. For vertical same adjacency (YY), intermediate block H, cost +1. So the extra internal cost due to hard passes is exactly min_same, for K=2. Same-axis adjacency cost depends on the orientation of the intermediate block, which alternates along the run. In a run of X moves, the intermediate blocks alternate V,H,V,H,... because block orientation toggles with each move. Horizontal pass through V is hard; horizontal pass through H is easy. - If A == B: number of runs is odd. If A != B: even.

   rX = rY + 1 if B=='X' else rX = rY? Let's derive:
   Sequence starts with X.

   If ends with Y: runs: X,Y,X,...,Y -> rX = rY.

   If ends with Y: rY = rX+1.
   If ends with X: rY = rX.
Given h,v, the number of runs is constrained: rX ≤ h (each run length≥1), rY ≤ v, and the above relation. We can choose rX,rY to minimize P.

If the block before the run is H, hard X passes = floor(r/2).
If before run is V, hard X passes = floor((r-1)/2) (as derived for r>=1; for r=1, 0).
Similarly for Y run:
If before run is V, hard Y passes = floor(r/2) (since V->H->V vertical pass through H hard).
If before run is H, hard Y passes = floor((r-1)/2).

The orientation before the first run is o_s. After a run of length r, orientation toggles r times, so the orientation before the next run is o_s xor (prefix length mod2). Thus it depends on the parity of the sum of lengths of previous runs. We can choose run lengths to minimize total P. This is a combinatorial optimization over run lengths with parity effects.

Claim: For K=2, there is an optimal path whose block projection is monotone (only moves in directions toward target, no opposite moves). Proof sketch: Any backtracking move (e.g., E then W) adds 2 block moves and returns to same block. It could be used to adjust k. So backtracking is never better than internal adjustment. Also backtracking to avoid hard passes costs 2, while a hard pass costs1, so not beneficial. Thus monotone optimal. Good.
