How to compute min P for given h,v,A,B,o_s?

Let's model the sequence of moves. A hard pass occurs on a pair of consecutive moves of the same axis if the intermediate block orientation is hard for that axis. Equivalently, for each adjacent pair of moves:
- If pair is X X: hard if the block after the first move is V.
- If pair is Y Y: hard if the block after the first move is H.

The block orientation after a move toggles. Let's define a binary sequence of orientations of blocks visited: b_0 = o_s (source block orientation), b_m = o_s xor (m mod2) after m moves. The move m goes from block m-1 to block m. A pair of moves m and m+1 are same axis. The intermediate block is block m. Hard if:
- axis X and b_m = V (1)
- axis Y and b_m = H (0)

So P = sum over m=1..L-1 of [move_m == move_{m+1} and ( (axis X and b_m=1) or (axis Y and b_m=0) )].

Since b_m = o_s xor (m mod2), the hard condition depends on m parity and axis.

Let's denote o_s = 0 for H, 1 for V.
For a same-axis pair at positions m,m+1:
- If axis X: hard if b_m = 1. b_m = o_s xor (m mod2). So hard if (o_s + m) mod2 = 1.
- If axis Y: hard if b_m = 0. hard if (o_s + m) mod2 = 0.

Thus:
- X X pair at m is hard iff m % 2 != o_s? Since (o_s + m)%2=1 => m%2 = 1-o_s.
- Y Y pair at m is hard iff m%2 = o_s.

Now, the sequence of moves is a binary string of length L with h X and v Y. We need count adjacent equal pairs weighted by position parity and axis.

This is a combinatorial optimization. We can think of constructing the string to minimize weighted adjacencies. Since weights depend on position parity and the character, we can perhaps compute min P via dynamic programming over counts and last character, but h,v huge. However the cost is linear and the optimal string likely alternates as much as possible, with extra runs placed at positions where the weight is 0 (not hard). We can derive formula.

Let's define for each possible adjacent equal pair:
- At odd m (1-indexed? Let's use 0-indexed positions i=0..L-2 for pair between move i and i+1. Block after move i is b_i = o_s xor ((i+1) mod2)? Wait if move index m from1 to L, block after m moves has orientation o_s xor (m mod2). Pair between move m and m+1 intermediate block after move m, orientation b_m = o_s xor (m mod2). Let's use m 1-indexed.
Hard:
XX hard if (o_s + m) %2 =1.
YY hard if (o_s + m)%2=0.

Equivalently, for a pair at position m:
- If m parity = 1-o_s, XX hard.
- If m parity = o_s, YY hard.
So at a given position m, one of the two same-axis pairs is hard depending on m parity and o_s:
If o_s=0 (H):
  m odd: XX hard (since 1), YY not.
  m even: YY hard, XX not.
If o_s=1 (V):
  m odd: YY hard? o_s=1, YY hard if m%2=1; XX hard if m%2=0.
  m even: XX hard.

Thus the "hard axis" at position m alternates with m. To minimize P, we want same-axis adjacencies to occur at positions where that axis is not hard, if possible.

This is similar to arranging a string with given counts to minimize weighted adjacencies. We can compute min P by considering the number of runs. In a string, the number of adjacencies of X is h - rX, where rX is number of X runs. Similarly Y adjacencies = v - rY. We need assign these adjacencies to positions. The positions of adjacencies of an axis are within runs; for a run of length r, it contributes r-1 adjacencies at consecutive positions. The parity of positions of adjacencies within a run depends on the run's start position.

Maybe we can compute minimal P by dynamic programming over the start of each run, but h,v huge. However, since weights are periodic with period 2, the minimal P can be expressed in terms of h,v and the number of runs. Let's derive.

Consider a run of X of length r starting at position m0 (1-indexed first move of run). It contributes r-1 XX adjacencies at positions m0, m0+1, ..., m0+r-2. Among these, the number of positions where XX is hard is:
- If o_s=0: hard at odd m. Count of odd numbers in interval [m0, m0+r-2].
- If o_s=1: hard at even m.
This count is either floor((r-1)/2) or ceil((r-1)/2) depending on start parity.
Similarly Y run hard count depends on o_s.

Thus total P = sum over X runs of hard_count_X(start, length) + sum over Y runs of hard_count_Y(start, length).

The start positions of runs alternate and are determined by previous run lengths. This is complex but maybe we can choose run lengths to make starts favorable.

Maybe we can use a dynamic programming formula over counts and start/end axes. Since h,v can be huge but the cost is linear, we can compute min P by considering the number of runs and using the fact that hard passes occur every other same-axis adjacency in runs. The total P for a sequence can be expressed in terms of the number of runs and the parity of run lengths.

Let's derive P in terms of runs and starting orientation.

Consider the sequence of moves. Let's define for each run of X of length r. The number of XX adjacencies in this run is r-1. They occur at positions m0, m0+1, ..., m0+r-2 where m0 is the position of the first move of the run. The hard condition for XX at position m is m%2 != o_s (since o_s=0 -> odd; o_s=1 -> even). So the number of hard XX in the run is the count of integers in [m0, m0+r-2] with parity != o_s.

Similarly for Y run, hard YY at position m has m%2 == o_s.

The start position m0 of a run depends on the lengths of previous runs. The parity of m0 is determined by the parity of the total number of previous moves. This is complex, but we can choose run lengths to minimize.

However, maybe we can compute minimal P using a small DP over the last run and counts modulo? Since h,v up to 4e11, but the number of runs is at most h+v. We need closed form.

Let's consider the problem of minimizing P over all binary strings with h X, v Y, fixed first axis A and last axis B. This is a 1D optimization with periodic weights. Maybe we can compute P by considering the sequence of axes only, not directions. The hard pass count for a run depends on the parity of its start position and the axis. The start parity of a run is determined by the parity of the sum of previous run lengths. Since we can choose run lengths, we can control start parity. The cost of a run of length r starting at parity p:
For X run:
- hard count = number of positions m in [start, start+r-2] with m%2 != o_s.
This is approximately (r-1)/2, with exact depending on start parity.
Specifically, if o_s=0 (H):
  XX hard at odd m.
  If start parity p (1-indexed):
    - p odd: positions start, start+1,...; odd count = ceil((r-1)/2)? Let's compute r=2, start odd: interval [odd, odd] count1 = ceil(1/2)=1. r=3, start odd: [odd, even] count1 = ceil(2/2)=1. r=4, start odd: [odd, even, odd] count2 = ceil(3/2)=2. So ceil((r-1)/2).
    - p even: odd count = floor((r-1)/2).
  For o_s=1, XX hard at even m, so p even -> ceil, p odd -> floor.
For Y run with o_s=0, YY hard at even m:
  p even -> ceil((r-1)/2), p odd -> floor.
For o_s=1, YY hard at odd m:
  p odd -> ceil, p even -> floor.

Thus the cost of a run depends on start parity and axis. The start parity of the next run is start parity + length of current run mod2. So we can model runs as a sequence with states parity. We want to minimize total cost given total h,v and first/last axis. This is a finite-state DP but with large run lengths. However, the cost function is roughly (r-1)/2 plus a small parity correction. The total cost over all runs is:
Sum over X runs (r_i -1)/2 + parity corrections = (h - rX)/2 + corrections.
Similarly Y: (v - rY)/2 + corrections.
Total P = (h+v - rX - rY)/2 + corrections.
Since h+v is fixed, minimizing P is roughly maximizing rX+rY (number of runs) and minimizing corrections. The number of runs is maximized by alternating as much as possible, i.e., rX and rY differ by at most1 and are limited by h,v. Specifically, max rX = min(h, v+1) if start/end X? But also rX ≤ h, rY ≤ v. The maximum total runs is:
- If h >= v: max rX = v+1, rY = v (if start/end X) or rX=v, rY=v (if start/end different? Actually if start X end Y, rX=rY ≤ v; max rX=rY=v if h,v >= v. If start/end X, rX=rY+1, max rX=v+1,rY=v.)
So total runs max = h? Let's not.

The parity correction is at most number of runs, so small. Maybe the minimal P is exactly floor(|h-v|/2) adjusted by start orientation and first/last axis? Let's compute examples:
o_s=0:
h=2,v=0, start/end X: P=1 = floor(2/2)=1.
h=4,v=0: P=2 = floor(4/2)=2.

So P=floor(v/2)? v=4 ->2? floor(4/2)=2, but actual1. So not.

For a single run of length L starting at p=1:
- X run, o_s=0: hard count = ceil((L-1)/2). For L=2 ->1, L=4->2.

Maybe we can handle K=2 by using the same K>2 algorithm but with a different "base" cost that is the minimum over monotone paths of h+v+P. Let's compute min P for given h,v, start/end axes using a small DP over the number of runs and parity, with O(1) formula.

Let's formulate the run-level problem.

A sequence is determined by:
- Number of X runs rX, Y runs rY.
- Start axis A, end axis B.
- The sequence of run axes alternates, so rX and rY are fixed by A,B:
  If A='X':
    if B='X': rX = rY + 1
    else: rX = rY
  If A='Y':
    if B='Y': rY = rX + 1
    else: rY = rX
Let r = min(rX,rY). The other is r or r+1.

Given rX,rY, we need distribute h X moves into rX positive integer run lengths, and v Y moves into rY positive run lengths. The cost P is sum of run costs depending on start parity. The start parity of the first run is 1 (first move position 1). After each run of length len, the parity for next run toggles if len odd.

We can choose run lengths to minimize cost. This is like we have a sequence of runs; each run has a base cost roughly (len-1)/2 plus parity correction. The total base cost over all runs is (h - rX)/2 + (v - rY)/2 (since sum(len-1)/2). The parity corrections depend on the parities of run lengths and start parities.

Let's compute the parity correction precisely.

For a run of axis X starting at parity p (1 for odd, 0 for even), length r:
Cost_X(p,r) = number of m in [p, p+r-2] with m%2 != o_s (using 1=odd).
Let q = o_s (0 H,1 V). Hard parity for X is 1-q (odd if H, even if V).
The count of integers in an interval of length r-1 starting at p with a given parity. This can be written as:
If (r-1) = 0: cost0.
Else cost = floor((r-1 + (p%2 == target? maybe)) /2).
Specifically, count of target parity in interval [p, p+L-1] where L=r-1.
Number = floor(L/2) + (1 if (p%2 == target and L%2==1?) Let's derive:
For L consecutive integers starting at p, count of parity target:
- If L even: exactly L/2.
- If L odd: (L+1)/2 if p parity == target, else (L-1)/2.
So cost_X = L//2 + (L%2==1 and p%2==target ? 1 : 0), where L=r-1, target = 1-o_s.
For Y run, target = o_s (hard parity for Y is o_s). Cost_Y similarly with target=o_s.

Base (r-1)//2 = L//2. The correction is 1 if L odd and p parity == target.

Thus total P = sum over runs (len-1)//2 + corrections = (h - rX)//2 + (v - rY)//2 + C, where C is the number of runs with odd length (len-1 odd => len even? Wait L=len-1 odd means len even) and start parity == target. More precisely, for each run, correction = 1 if len is even and start parity == target_axis. If len odd, L even, correction0.

So C counts runs of even length whose start parity equals the hard parity for that axis.

We can choose run lengths (positive integers summing to h for X, v for Y) to minimize C. The base term is fixed once rX,rY chosen. We can also choose rX,rY (within constraints) to minimize total.

This is now a combinatorial problem of choosing run lengths parities. Since only parity of run lengths matters for C, and the base term depends on rX,rY.

Let's denote:
- For X runs, we need rX positive integers summing to h. Let eX = number of even-length X runs. The parity of h is congruent to rX - eX mod2? Sum parity = (rX - eX) mod2 (odd runs count parity). So eX ≡ rX - h (mod2). eX can range from max(0, rX - h) to floor(rX/2)? Actually number of even runs has same parity as rX - h and ≤ rX.
- Similarly eY ≡ rY - v (mod2).

The start parity of each run is determined by the parity of previous run lengths. The sequence of start parities alternates based on whether previous run length was odd/even. This is like a path where state is current start parity. We need count of runs where start parity == target_axis and length even.

This is still complex but maybe we can minimize C by choosing parities appropriately. Since h,v huge, the optimal will use as few even runs as needed to satisfy parity, and place them to avoid corrections if possible.

Maybe we can avoid K=2 special by using a generic Dijkstra on a finite graph with coordinates modulo 2 and using the fact that K=2 distance is the shortest path in a weighted grid with edge weights 1 and "diagonal" costs? Let's try to map K=2 to a standard grid.

Let's not spend too long. We can implement K=2 with a BFS-like formula using the run DP and maybe it's acceptable.

Let's derive min P for K=2 using a small dynamic programming over run counts and parities with O(1) formulas.

Since h,v can be huge, but the number of runs rX,rY is at most min(h,v)+1. We can choose rX,rY to minimize total cost. The base term (h-rX)//2 + (v-rY)//2 decreases as rX+rY increases, so we want maximum rX+rY. The maximum rX,rY are determined by start/end axes and h,v:
- If start/end axes are different (A!=B): rX = rY = r. Max r = min(h,v). (Because runs alternate starting with A ending with B, equal number of each.)
- If start=end=X: rX = rY + 1. Max rY = min(v, h-1) (since rX≤h, rY≤v). So rY = min(v, h-1), rX = rY+1. If h=0 impossible.

Given time, perhaps we can implement K=2 by running a BFS on a small graph of "macro" moves using the fact that the distance is the minimum over at most 4 axis patterns of h+v+P, and compute P for each pattern by a simple formula:

For h=2,v=4, o_s=H:
X contribution: h=2, v=4, X is minority. X runs count depends on pattern:
- A=Y,B=Y: rX=2, X runs length1 each, P_X=0.
- A=X,B=X: rX=2? Wait start/end X with h=2,v=4 gave rX=2, X runs length1, P_X=0. P came from Y. So P_X=0 always because h small.
Y contribution:
- A=Y,B=Y: rY=3, Y excess over runs? v=4, rY=3, one YY adjacency. Its position can be chosen not hard ->0.
- A=X,B=X: rY=1, v=4, three YY adjacencies in one run, hard count2.

From H1:
- E to V0: disp (1,0), state V0
- W to V1: disp (-1,0), state V1
- N to V0/V1: disp (0,1), state V0 or V1
- S not allowed
- internal to H0: disp (0,0)
From V0:
- N to H0: disp (0,1), state H0
- S to H1: disp (0,-1), state H1
- W to H0/H1: disp (-1,0), state H0 or H1
- E not allowed (k=0)
- internal to V1: disp (0,0)
From V1:
- N to H0: disp (0,1), state H0
- S to H1: disp (0,-1), state H1
- E to H0/H1: disp (1,0), state H0 or H1
- W not allowed
- internal to V0: disp (0,0)

This is a finite automaton with 4 states and moves with displacement in Z^2. We need the shortest path from source state to target state with total displacement (ΔI,ΔJ). This is a "shortest path in a weighted automaton" which can be solved by computing the minimum cost to achieve displacement using min-plus matrix exponentiation over the displacement? Since displacement is 2D and large, but the automaton is small. The set of achievable displacements per cost forms a convex polygon. The distance is the minimum cost c such that (ΔI,ΔJ) is in the c-step reachability set. This can be computed by finding the convex hull of the "average displacement" of cycles. For a finite automaton, the growth is linear, and the distance is the support function of a convex set. We could compute it by linear programming over cycles, but implementing is hard.

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys

def floor_div(a, b):
    return a // b

def solve_case(K, Sx, Sy, Tx, Ty):
    Is = Sx // K
    Js = Sy // K
    It = Tx // K
    Jt = Ty // K

    os = (Is + Js) & 1  # 0: H, 1: V
    ot = (It + Jt) & 1

    if os == 0:
        ks = Sy - Js * K
    else:
        ks = Sx - Is * K

    if ot == 0:
        kt = Ty - Jt * K
    else:
        kt = Tx - It * K

    # Same block
    if Is == It and Js == Jt:
        return min(abs(ks - kt), 2)

    h = abs(It - Is)
    v = abs(Jt - Js)
    sh = 1 if It > Is else -1 if It < Is else 0
    sv = 1 if Jt > Js else -1 if Jt < Js else 0

    M = max(h, v)
    delta = 0 if os == ot else 1
    B = 2 * M - delta

    if delta == 0:
        nX = M
        nY = M
    else:
        if h > v:
            nX = M
            nY = M - 1
        else:
            nX = M - 1
            nY = M

    e = (nX + sh * h) // 2
    w = (nX - sh * h) // 2
    nn = (nY + sv * v) // 2
    ss = (nY - sv * v) // 2

    patterns = []
    if nX == nY:
        patterns.append(('X', 'Y'))
        patterns.append(('Y', 'X'))
    elif nX == nY + 1:
        patterns.append(('X', 'X'))
    else:
        patterns.append(('Y', 'Y'))

    def feasible(ax1, axL, d1, dL):
        cntX = {'E': e, 'W': w}
        cntY = {'N': nn, 'S': ss}

        if ax1 == 'X':
            if cntX[d1] < 1:
                return False
        else:
            if cntY[d1] < 1:
                return False

        if axL == 'X':
            if ax1 == 'X' and nX == 1:
                if d1 != dL:
                    return False
                if cntX[d1] < 1:
                    return False
            else:
                if cntX[dL] < 1:
                    return False
                if ax1 == 'X' and d1 == dL and nX > 1 and cntX[d1] < 2:
                    return False
        else:
            if ax1 == 'Y' and nY == 1:
                if d1 != dL:
                    return False
                if cntY[d1] < 1:
                    return False
            else:
                if cntY[dL] < 1:
                    return False
                if ax1 == 'Y' and d1 == dL and nY > 1 and cntY[d1] < 2:
                    return False
        return True

    def source_penalty(o, k, d):
        if o == 0:  # H
            if d == 'N':
                req = K - 1
            elif d == 'S':
                req = 0
            else:
                return 0
        else:       # V
            if d == 'E':
                req = K - 1
            elif d == 'W':
                req = 0
            else:
                return 0
        return min(abs(k - req), 2)

    def target_penalty(o, k, d):
        if o == 0:  # H
            if d == 'N':
                req = 0
            elif d == 'S':
                req = K - 1
            else:
                return 0
        else:       # V
            if d == 'E':
                req = 0
            elif d == 'W':
                req = K - 1
            else:
                return 0
        return min(abs(k - req), 2)

    best_pen = 10**30

    for ax1, axL in patterns:
        if ax1 == 'X':
            start_dirs = []
            if e > 0:
                start_dirs.append('E')
            if w > 0:
                start_dirs.append('W')
        else:
            start_dirs = []
            if nn > 0:
                start_dirs.append('N')
            if ss > 0:
                start_dirs.append('S')

        if axL == 'X':
            end_dirs = []
            if e > 0:
                end_dirs.append('E')
            if w > 0:
                end_dirs.append('W')
        else:
            end_dirs = []
            if nn > 0:
                end_dirs.append('N')
            if ss > 0:
                end_dirs.append('S')

        for d1 in start_dirs:
            for dL in end_dirs:
                if feasible(ax1, axL, d1, dL):
                    pen = source_penalty(os, ks, d1) + target_penalty(ot, kt, dL)
                    if pen < best_pen:
                        best_pen = pen

    return B + best_pen


def main():
    data = sys.stdin.read().strip().split()
    if not data:
        return
    T = int(data[0])
    out = []
    idx = 1
    for _ in range(T):
        K = int(data[idx]); Sx = int(data[idx+1]); Sy = int(data[idx+2])
        Tx = int(data[idx+3]); Ty = int(data[idx+4])
        idx += 5
        out.append(str(solve_case(K, Sx, Sy, Tx, Ty)))
    print("\n".join(out))


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
